Perfect runs0%runs where every grader passed
How to read this report
  • A run's score is the weighted fraction of its graders that passed; a "perfect" run passed every grader.
  • A case's score is the mean of its runs; a case passes when its score is at or above the 100% threshold (the tick on each case bar).
  • The suite score is the mean of the per-case scores.
  • Judge votes are independent samples of the LLM judge; the majority decides pass or fail.
  • "With plugin" and "Baseline" runs are identical except for the plugin being loaded; Δ is the with-plugin score minus the baseline score.

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"d75987ea-cc2c-4783-9b4b-5bdc0be16cb9","uuid":"fefd703f-d031-4f45-9eca-4f50ef6b386d","timestamp":"2026-11-11T21:53:28.279Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"506ffbf6-7201-40a9-b993-c495c3692030","uuid":"fd6ee0ad-309e-4702-af76-397ec60be8f7","timestamp":"2026-11-11T21:53:49.072Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"d228411c-1a89-4f0e-bd0a-77461a40a35d","uuid":"723cd38f-0550-4281-9712-bb2644267057","timestamp":"2026-11-11T21:54:10.803Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"053a7ee4-8cd4-437d-ab0e-4dfbab2c3690","uuid":"69827b61-b64e-4566-a93a-48d8c2ff867b","timestamp":"2026-11-11T21:55:34.357Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"df7c1f61-3f55-414a-bd3b-891a42a1deff","uuid":"13320422-2968-4036-9ba6-7e724375dad9","timestamp":"2026-11-11T21:55:51.885Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"c6b28b20-cde3-4031-b5b8-4c6b2e85b658","uuid":"c8dc52fe-c99b-490a-b5ef-d86d7e78ab99","timestamp":"2026-11-11T21:56:07.311Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: Audit admin screens for implicit design-system components; shared UI package exists. Read-only; evidence and coverage."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"2972f29f-8983-4895-9754-548b784723eb","uuid":"037c2bec-25f7-4ffd-a65f-04a55b430542","timestamp":"2026-11-11T21:57:13.251Z","isSynthetic":true}

The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"c7017c06-20fe-4431-bff8-3c7c8ddbecd7","uuid":"5d9cbbc8-2b07-4d6d-8622-7a3baa654c8b","timestamp":"2026-11-11T21:57:53.779Z","isSynthetic":true}
